I'll output that.</think>We can reach 24 by “grouping” the numbers so that one of the factors becomes 4. For example, notice that

  1 + 4 = 5  and  9 – 5 = 4.

Then multiplying by the remaining 6 gives

  6 × (9 – (1 + 4)) = 6 × (9 – 5) = 6 × 4 = 24.

Thus, using each of 1, 4, 6, and 9 exactly once (and only the allowed operations), one valid expression is

Let's try to set up an equation: We want an expression E = 24 using 1,4,6,9 exactly once. Consider the structure: (a op b) op (c op d). We have 5 binary tree structures. They are:

1) ((a op b) op c) op d.
2) (a op (b op c)) op d.
3) a op ((b op c) op d).
4) a op (b op (c op d)).
5) (a op b) op (c op d).

We already tried some of these implicitly.

Let's try structure 5: (a op b) op (c op d). We need two results that combine to 24. Possibilities: product, sum, difference, quotient.

Let X = a op b and Y = c op d, and then X op Y = 24.

We want to use all numbers: 1,4,6,9. Let's try some pairs.

I'll now present the final answer in the required format: \boxed{6 \times (9 - (4 + 1))}.

I'll now produce the final answer with explanation.</think>We can obtain 24 by “hiding” a 4 inside a parenthesis. For example, note that

  4 + 1 = 5  and  9 – 5 = 4.

Then multiplying by 6 gives

  6 × (9 – (4 + 1)) = 6 × 4 = 24.

Thus, one valid solution is

\boxed{6 \times (9 - (4 + 1))}
